Acquainted. Here's where things stand:

**Project**: `vc-x1` v0.41.1-6.7 — Rust dual-repo (jj-git) workspace tool. Single binary with subcommands (`init`, `clone`, `push`, `sync`, `chid`, `desc`, etc.). Two coexisting repos: app (`.`) and bot session (`.claude`).

**Repo state**: Both repos clean at the close-out of sub-step `0.41.1-6.7` ("OF refactor cycle"). Working copies are empty descriptions on top of those close-out commits. App is on bookmark `init-clone-refactor`; `.claude` is on `main`.

**In-flight cycle**: `0.41.1-6` (init refactor + symmetric `.vc-config.toml` schema). Through `-6.7` done (8 sub-sub-step commits + close-out kept separate, not squashed, since the decomposition itself was informative). Next: `-6.8` — `init_with_symlink` rename + `InitDualArgs`/`InitPorArgs` split via `#[command(flatten)]` of the common bundle that `-6.7` built. CLI surface decision (subcommands vs preserved `--scope` flag) is deferred to `-6.8` design time.

**Workflow**: `vc-x1 push <bookmark>` is the primary commit-push-finalize tool, with two approval gates (review, message). `notes/todo.md` carries the in-progress ladder; `notes/chores-08.md` owns the design for the active cycle.

Ready when you are.

79	/// Run a command with retries, sleeping between attempts.
80	fn run_retry(
81	    cmd: &str,
82	    args: &[&str],
83	    cwd: &Path,
84	    retry: &PushRetryOptions,
85	) -> Result<String, Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
86	    let mut last_err = String::new();
87	    for attempt in 1..=retry.push_retries {
88	        match run(cmd, args, cwd) {
89	            Ok(out) => {
90	                if attempt > 1 {
91	                    debug!("succeeded after {attempt} attempts");
92	                }
93	                return Ok(out);
94	            }
95	            Err(e) => {
96	                last_err = e.to_string();
97	                if attempt < retry.push_retries {
98	                    debug!(
99	                        "attempt {attempt}/{} failed: {last_err}",
100	                        retry.push_retries
101	                    );
102	                    debug!("retrying in {}s...", retry.push_retry_delay);
103	                    std::thread::sleep(std::time::Duration::from_secs(retry.push_retry_delay));
104	                }
105	            }
106	        }
107	    }
108	    Err(format!("failed after {} attempts: {last_err}", retry.push_retries).into())
109	}

> AGENT

Two observations after looking at the code:

**Your "simplest" is roughly what's there today.** `init_with_symlink(&args, bool)` already takes the toggle (init.rs:968) — `init()` is just a 1-line wrapper passing `true` (init.rs:959–961), tests call `init_with_symlink(&args, false)` directly. Collapsing to one function with the bool exposed is a net change of maybe three lines.

**Putting `create_symlink` in `InitArgs` mixes layers.** `InitArgs` is the clap-derived CLI surface; `create_symlink=false` is a test-harness suppression. Adding it as `#[arg(skip)]` works mechanically but plants a "this is for tests" field in the user-facing struct, and it's harder to defend than a plain function parameter that says exactly "the CLI does the symlink, the tests don't."

**The Dual/Por split is a separate question.** Planned `-6.8` was about per-scope CLI surfaces (e.g., `--config` only meaningful for POR — currently rejected in preflight, which clap could reject at parse time if the args were split). That's orthogonal to the symlink toggle. You can drop the split independently if you want, but the symlink question doesn't drive it either way.

Recommendation: keep the symlink as a function parameter (collapse `init`/`init_with_symlink` into one public `init(args, create_symlink)` if the dual entry point bothers you), and decide Dual/Por split on its own merits — what's the user-facing pain it solves?

1	//! Shared test helpers for dual-repo integration tests.
2	//!
3	//! Provides a `Fixture` that wraps `crate::init::init_with_symlink`
4	//! plus a per-process unique-tempdir helper so parallel tests don't
5	//! collide. Lifted out of the `sync` test module (originally inline at
6	//! `sync.rs:521–560`) so `push`'s tests (0.37.0) and any future
7	//! subcommand's tests can sit on the same harness without
8	//! copy-paste drift.
9	//!
10	//! Test-only — the whole module is gated at its declaration site via
11	//! `#[cfg(test)] mod test_helpers;`.
12	
13	use std::fs;
14	use std::path::PathBuf;
15	use std::sync::atomic::{AtomicU64, Ordering};
16	use std::time::{SystemTime, UNIX_EPOCH};
17	
18	use crate::common::write_file;
19	use crate::config::RepoSelector;
20	use crate::init::{InitArgs, init_with_symlink};
21	use crate::options_flags::account::AccountOption;
22	use crate::options_flags::config::ConfigOption;
23	use crate::options_flags::provision_bundle::ProvisionOptionFlagBundle;
24	use crate::options_flags::repo::RepoOption;
25	use crate::options_flags::scope::{ScopeKind, ScopeOption};
26	use crate::options_flags::use_template::UseTemplateOption;
27	use crate::test_tmp_root::{resolve_tmp_root, should_keep_tempdir};
28	
29	/// Per-process counter so same-nanosecond tempdir collisions yield
30	/// distinct paths when tests run in parallel.
31	static COUNTER: AtomicU64 = AtomicU64::new(0);
32	
33	/// Build a unique tempdir path for a test fixture.
34	///
35	/// Combines a nanosecond timestamp with a per-process atomic counter
36	/// so parallel tests and same-nanosecond collisions both yield
37	/// distinct paths. Prefix is `vc-x1-test-<tag>-<ts>-<n>` so callers
38	/// from different subcommand tests stay discoverable in `$TMPDIR`.
39	pub fn unique_base(tag: &str) -> PathBuf {
40	    let ts = SystemTime::now()
41	        .duration_since(UNIX_EPOCH)
42	        .map(|d| d.as_nanos())
43	        .unwrap_or(0); // OK: clock error → 0 is harmless for unique tempdir naming
44	    let n = COUNTER.fetch_add(1, Ordering::SeqCst);
45	    resolve_tmp_root().join(format!("vc-x1-test-{tag}-{ts}-{n}"))
46	}
47	
48	/// Owned dual-repo fixture with RAII cleanup.
49	///
50	/// Builds a fresh throwaway dual-repo set under a unique tempdir
51	/// by driving `init::init_with_symlink` with a path TARGET and
52	/// `--repo local=<base>` (so config lookup is skipped via the
53	/// resolve_repo short-circuit). `create_symlink=false` suppresses
54	/// the `~/.claude/projects/` side effect. Exposes the two repo
55	/// paths (`work` and `work/.claude`). The tempdir tree is removed
56	/// when the value is dropped, so a panicking test still cleans up
57	/// after itself.
58	pub struct Fixture {
59	    /// Root tempdir that owns both repos and their bare-git remotes.
60	    pub base: PathBuf,
61	    /// Code repo path (`<base>/work`).
62	    pub work: PathBuf,
63	    /// Bot session repo path (`<base>/work/.claude`).
64	    pub claude: PathBuf,
65	}
66	
67	impl Fixture {
68	    /// Build a fresh fixture in a unique tempdir.
69	    pub fn new(tag: &str) -> Self {
70	        Self::new_opts(tag, false, None)
71	    }
72	
73	    /// Build a fresh fixture with optional pending changes and a
74	    /// template seed.
75	    ///
76	    /// - `with_pending` — after init, write a small file into each
77	    ///   repo so `@` carries uncommitted changes (useful for
78	    ///   finalize / push tests).
79	    /// - `use_template` — `CODE[,BOT]` forwarded to init.
80	    pub fn new_opts(tag: &str, with_pending: bool, use_template: Option<String>) -> Self {
81	        let base = unique_base(tag);
82	        // init refuses to reuse an existing project_dir, so `base`
83	        // must not exist yet. `unique_base` already guarantees that.
84	        //
85	        // Path TARGET = `<base>/work` (workspace destination); the
86	        // basename ("work") becomes the repo name. `--repo local=<base>`
87	        // sets the bare-repo parent so the layout mirrors the old
88	        // `--repo-local <base>` + NAME=`work` shape:
89	        //   <base>/work/                  (code repo)
90	        //   <base>/work/.claude/          (bot session repo)
91	        //   <base>/remote-code.git        (code bare origin)
92	        //   <base>/remote-claude.git      (bot bare origin)
93	        let work_path = base.join("work");
94	        let args = InitArgs {
95	            target: work_path.to_string_lossy().into_owned(),
96	            name: None,
97	            account: AccountOption::default(),
98	            repo: RepoOption {
99	                repo: Some(RepoSelector {
100	                    category: "local".to_string(),
101	                    value: Some(base.to_string_lossy().into_owned()),
102	                }),
103	            },
104	            scope: ScopeOption {
105	                scope: ScopeKind::CodeBot,
106	            },
107	            provision: ProvisionOptionFlagBundle::default(),
108	            use_template: UseTemplateOption { use_template },
109	            config: ConfigOption::default(),
110	        };
111	        init_with_symlink(&args, false).expect("build test fixture via init");
112	
113	        let work = base.join("work");
114	        let claude = work.join(".claude");
115	
116	        if with_pending {
117	            write_file(&work.join("TODO.md"), "# TODO\n- first feature\n")
118	                .expect("write pending TODO.md");
119	            write_file(
120	                &claude.join("session-notes.md"),
121	                "# Session notes\n- simulated pending work\n",
122	            )
123	            .expect("write pending session-notes.md");
124	        }
125	
126	        Fixture { base, work, claude }
127	    }
128	
129	    /// Convenience: return both repo paths as a `Vec<PathBuf>`
130	    /// suitable for `sync_repos` (or any other `&[PathBuf]` API).
131	    pub fn repos(&self) -> Vec<PathBuf> {
132	        vec![self.work.clone(), self.claude.clone()]
133	    }
134	}
135	
136	impl Drop for Fixture {
137	    /// Remove the fixture tree on drop. Best-effort; a failure here
138	    /// doesn't fail the test. Suppressed when `$VC_X1_TEST_KEEP` is
139	    /// set — see `test_tmp_root::should_keep_tempdir`.
140	    fn drop(&mut self) {
141	        if should_keep_tempdir() {
142	            eprintln!("VC_X1_TEST_KEEP set; preserving {}", self.base.display());
143	        } else {
144	            let _ = fs::remove_dir_all(&self.base);
145	        }
146	    }
147	}
148	
149	/// Owned single-repo (POR) fixture with RAII cleanup.
150	///
151	/// Sibling of `Fixture` for `--scope=por` flows. Drives
152	/// `init::init_with_symlink` with `ScopeKind::Por` and a path
153	/// TARGET; `--repo local=<base>` steers the bare origin to
154	/// `<base>/remote.git` (vs. dual's `remote-code.git` /
155	/// `remote-claude.git`). No `.claude/` peer, no symlink.
156	///
157	/// Field shape differs from `Fixture` — there is no `claude` peer
158	/// path — so it's a distinct type rather than an `Option<PathBuf>`
159	/// on `Fixture` (the latter would force every dual-using caller to
160	/// `.unwrap()` or pattern-match).
161	pub struct FixturePor {
162	    /// Root tempdir that owns the repo and its bare-git remote.
163	    pub base: PathBuf,
164	    /// POR repo path (`<base>/work`).
165	    pub work: PathBuf,
166	}
167	
168	impl FixturePor {
169	    /// Build a fresh POR fixture in a unique tempdir.
170	    pub fn new(tag: &str) -> Self {
171	        Self::new_with_config(tag, None)
172	    }
173	
174	    /// Build a fresh POR fixture, threading `config` through to
175	    /// `InitArgs` for `--config` variant testing.
176	    pub fn new_with_config(tag: &str, config: Option<String>) -> Self {
177	        let base = unique_base(tag);
178	        // Path TARGET = `<base>/work`; basename ("work") becomes
179	        // the repo name. `--repo local=<base>` sets the bare-repo
180	        // parent, producing the layout:
181	        //   <base>/work/        (POR repo)
182	        //   <base>/remote.git   (bare origin)
183	        let work_path = base.join("work");
184	        let args = InitArgs {
185	            target: work_path.to_string_lossy().into_owned(),
186	            name: None,
187	            account: AccountOption::default(),
188	            repo: RepoOption {
189	                repo: Some(RepoSelector {
190	                    category: "local".to_string(),
191	                    value: Some(base.to_string_lossy().into_owned()),
192	                }),
193	            },
194	            scope: ScopeOption {
195	                scope: ScopeKind::Por,
196	            },
197	            provision: ProvisionOptionFlagBundle::default(),
198	            use_template: UseTemplateOption::default(),
199	            config: ConfigOption { raw: config },
200	        };
201	        init_with_symlink(&args, false).expect("build test fixture via init (POR)");
202	
203	        let work = base.join("work");
204	        FixturePor { base, work }
205	    }
206	}
207	
208	impl Drop for FixturePor {
209	    /// Remove the fixture tree on drop. Best-effort; a failure here
210	    /// doesn't fail the test. Suppressed when `$VC_X1_TEST_KEEP` is
211	    /// set — see `test_tmp_root::should_keep_tempdir`.
212	    fn drop(&mut self) {
213	        if should_keep_tempdir() {
214	            eprintln!("VC_X1_TEST_KEEP set; preserving {}", self.base.display());
215	        } else {
216	            let _ = fs::remove_dir_all(&self.base);
217	        }
218	    }
219	}

34	/// Banner string emitted as the first line of normal command runs
35	/// and shown at the top of subcommand `--help` output. Built from
36	/// `Cargo.toml`'s name + version at compile time so it stays in
37	/// sync with the bumped version.
38	const BANNER: &str = concat!(env!("CARGO_PKG_NAME"), " ", env!("CARGO_PKG_VERSION"));
39	
40	/// Top-level about line — name, version, and the project tagline
41	/// on a single line. Used as the top-level `about` so `vc-x1 -h`
42	/// reads as one banner-plus-tagline header instead of two stacked
43	/// lines.
44	const TOP_ABOUT: &str = concat!(
45	    env!("CARGO_PKG_NAME"),
46	    " ",
47	    env!("CARGO_PKG_VERSION"),
48	    " - jj workspace tooling"
49	);
50	
51	/// Build the clap command tree with `BANNER` set as `before_help`
52	/// on every subcommand (transitively). Top-level skips `before_help`
53	/// because its own `about` already carries the name+version+tagline.
54	/// Walks via `mut_subcommand` so individual subcommand
55	/// `#[command(long_about = ...)]` blocks don't have to repeat the
56	/// banner text.
57	fn cli_with_banner() -> clap::Command {
58	    fn add_to_subs(mut cmd: clap::Command) -> clap::Command {
59	        let names: Vec<String> = cmd
60	            .get_subcommands()
61	            .map(|c| c.get_name().to_string())
62	            .collect();
63	        for name in names {
64	            cmd = cmd.mut_subcommand(name, add_with_banner);
65	        }
66	        cmd
67	    }
68	    fn add_with_banner(mut cmd: clap::Command) -> clap::Command {
69	        cmd = cmd.before_help(BANNER);
70	        let names: Vec<String> = cmd
71	            .get_subcommands()
72	            .map(|c| c.get_name().to_string())
73	            .collect();
74	        for name in names {
75	            cmd = cmd.mut_subcommand(name, add_with_banner);
76	        }
77	        cmd
78	    }
79	    add_to_subs(Cli::command())
80	}
81	
82	#[derive(Parser, Debug)]
83	#[command(
84	    version,
85	    propagate_version = true,
86	    about = TOP_ABOUT,
87	    max_term_width = 80
88	)]

90	    /// Verbose output: -v debug, -vv trace
91	    #[arg(short, long, global = true, action = clap::ArgAction::Count)]
92	    pub verbose: u8,
93	
94	    /// Log file path (captures all levels)
95	    #[arg(long, global = true)]
96	    pub log: Option<std::path::PathBuf>,
97	
98	    #[command(subcommand)]
99	    pub(crate) command: Commands,
100	}

103	pub(crate) enum Commands {
104	    /// Print the changeID for a revision
105	    Chid(chid::ChidArgs),
106	
107	    /// Show full description of a commit
108	    Desc(desc::DescArgs),
109	
110	    /// List commits in a jj repo
111	    List(list::ListArgs),
112	
113	    /// Show commit details and diff summary
114	    Show(show::ShowArgs),
115	
116	    /// Validate commit descriptions against the other repo

118	        long_about = "Validate commit descriptions against the other repo.\n\n\
119	        Output columns: STATUS  CHANGEID  TITLE  [DETAILS]\n\n\
120	        Status labels:\n  \
121	          ok   — ochid trailer is valid\n  \
122	          err  — ochid has issues (wrong prefix, wrong length, ID not found)\n  \
123	          miss — no ochid trailer; shows match from other repo if found"
124	    )]
125	    ValidateDesc(validate_desc::ValidateDescArgs),
126	
127	    /// Fix commit descriptions against the other repo (dry-run by default)
128	    #[command(long_about = "Fix commit descriptions against the other repo.\n\n\
129	        Default is dry-run; use --no-dry-run to write changes.\n\n\
130	        Output columns: STATUS  CHANGEID  TITLE  [DETAILS]\n\n\
131	        Status labels:\n  \
132	          ok    — ochid trailer is valid (no change)\n  \
133	          fix   — ochid has issues, shows proposed fix (dry-run)\n  \
134	          fixed — ochid was rewritten (--no-dry-run)\n  \
135	          add   — missing ochid, match found, shows proposed addition (dry-run)\n  \
136	          added — missing ochid was added (--no-dry-run)\n  \
137	          skip  — skipped (no ochid, no match, or max-fixes reached)\n  \
138	          err   — ID not found and no --fallback provided")]
139	    FixDesc(fix_desc::FixDescArgs),
140	
141	    /// Clone a dual-repo project
142	    Clone(clone::CloneArgs),
143	
144	    /// Create a new dual-repo project
145	    Init(init::InitArgs),
146	
147	    /// Create Claude Code project symlink
148	    Symlink(symlink::SymlinkArgs),
149	
150	    /// Fetch and sync a set of repos to their remotes
151	    #[command(long_about = "Fetch and sync a set of repos to their remotes.\n\n\
152	        Repo set is resolved (in order):\n  \
153	          - `-R` / `--repo`     exact list (back-compat / arbitrary multi-repo)\n  \
154	          - `--scope=code|bot|code,bot` dual-repo roles via `.vc-config.toml`\n  \
155	          - neither             default: `code,bot` when dual, else `code`\n\n\
156	        Default is --check (verify only) — fatal if any repo needs\n\
157	        action. Re-run with --no-check to apply (rebase / fast-forward).\n\
158	        Per repo:\n  \
159	          - up-to-date        nothing to do\n  \
160	          - behind            fast-forward bookmark to remote\n  \
161	          - ahead             nothing to sync (local has unpushed work)\n  \
162	          - diverged          rebase local onto remote; fail on conflicts\n  \
163	          - no remote         bookmark has no @<remote> counterpart; skip\n\n\
164	        Scripts and automation should pass --check or --no-check\n\
165	        explicitly — defaults can shift, explicit flags lock in the\n\
166	        contract. Interactive use can rely on the default.\n\n\
167	        After the bookmark action, `@` is rebased onto the (possibly\n\
168	        advanced) bookmark if it isn't already a descendant, so trailing\n\
169	        working-copy writes (e.g. `.claude`'s `/exit` tail) don't end up\n\
170	        orphaned on a stale branch.\n\n\
171	        On any failure, every repo is reverted to its starting state via\n\
172	        `jj op restore`. Working-copy files are preserved across the\n\
173	        revert — the operation log rewinds but disk content stays.\n\n\
174	        Output shape:\n  \
175	          - all-up-to-date: one-line summary (`sync: N repos, all bookmarks up-to-date`)\n  \
176	          - action needed (--check):  per-repo fetch + state + fatal error\n  \
177	          - action needed (--no-check): per-repo fetch + state + actions\n  \
178	          - --quiet:        no output; exit code signals success")]
179	    Sync(sync::SyncArgs),
180	
181	    /// Squash, set bookmark, and/or push a jj repo
182	    #[command(long_about = "Squash, set bookmark, and/or push a jj repo.\n\n\
183	        Designed for the bot to atomically finalize its session repo:\n\
184	        --detach exits immediately, --delay waits for trailing writes,\n\
185	        --squash folds them in, --bookmark + --push sends it upstream.\n\
186	        Every flag is opt-in. See README.md for details.")]
187	    Finalize(finalize::FinalizeArgs),
188	
189	    /// Dual-repo commit+push+finalize in one resumable command
190	    #[command(
191	        long_about = "Dual-repo commit+push+finalize in one resumable command.\n\n\
192	        Collapses today's manual Commit-Push-Finalize Flow into a\n\
193	        single subcommand with two interactive approval gates and a\n\
194	        state machine with persistent progress so interruptions can\n\
195	        resume without re-doing completed stages.\n\n\
196	        Stages: preflight (fmt/clippy/test) → review (approve diff)\n\
197	        → message ($EDITOR / --title+--body, approve text) →\n\
198	        commit-app → commit-claude (skipped if clean) → bookmark-both\n\
199	        → push-app → finalize-claude. Failures in commit-app /\n\
200	        commit-claude / bookmark-both roll both repos back via\n\
201	        `jj op restore` to the snapshot recorded before commit-app.\n\
202	        After push-app succeeds the remote boundary is crossed and\n\
203	        recovery is forward-only.\n\n\
204	        Non-interactive use: pass both --title and --body plus --yes\n\
205	        to skip the review gate. Saved state carries title/body\n\
206	        across resumes so only the first invocation needs them."
207	    )]
208	    Push(push::PushArgs),
209	}

211	fn run_command(result: Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>>) -> ExitCode {
212	    match result {
213	        Ok(()) => ExitCode::SUCCESS,
214	        Err(e) => {
215	            error!("{e}");
216	            ExitCode::FAILURE
217	        }
218	    }
219	}
220	
221	/// Permanent sanity check for the `main`-bookmark tracking state
222	/// in both repos of the dual-repo workspace. Prints one line on
223	/// entry and one on exit of every command. If entry and exit
224	/// differ, the executing command is the culprit; if entry differs
225	/// from the previous command's exit, something *between*
226	/// invocations broke it. Originally added in 0.37.2 as a temporary
227	/// diagnostic; promoted to permanent in 0.37.4 after the user
228	/// reported "happens more than once" — the signal is worth keeping
229	/// in place until confidence in its correctness is well-established
230	/// across varied use, at which point a "silent when clean"
231	/// refinement would remove the steady-state noise without losing
232	/// detection value (see `notes/todo.md`).
233	///
234	/// Walks up from cwd to locate the workspace root (the directory
235	/// whose `.vc-config.toml` has `path = "/"`), then probes `<root>`
236	/// and `<root>/.claude`. Same labeling whether the user runs from
237	/// the app root, from `.claude`, or from any subdir.
238	fn bm_track(phase: &str, command_name: &str) {
239	    let header = format!("bm-track {phase} vc-x1 {command_name}");
240	    let root = match common::find_workspace_root() {
241	        Some(r) => r,
242	        None => {
243	            log::info!("{header}: no-workspace");
244	            return;
245	        }
246	    };
247	    let repos: [(std::path::PathBuf, &str); 2] =
248	        [(root.clone(), "app"), (root.join(".claude"), ".claude")];
249	    let mut parts: Vec<String> = Vec::new();
250	    for (repo, label) in repos {
251	        if !repo.join(".jj").exists() {
252	            parts.push(format!("{label}(main)=no-jj"));
253	            continue;
254	        }
255	        match bm_track_one(&repo, "main", "origin") {
256	            Ok(true) => parts.push(format!("{label}(main)=tracked")),
257	            Ok(false) => parts.push(format!("{label}(main)=NOT_TRACKED")),
258	            Err(e) => parts.push(format!(
259	                "{label}(main)=err({})",
260	                e.lines().next().unwrap_or("")
261	            )),
262	        }
263	    }
264	    log::info!("{header}: {}", parts.join(", "));
265	}
266	
267	/// Query jj for whether `bookmark` in `repo` is tracking `remote`.
268	/// Returns `Ok(true)` if the tracked-list entry for `bookmark` shows
269	/// an `@<remote>` line (in any form — `@origin:` when synced, or
270	/// `@origin (ahead by N commits):` / similar when divergent — both
271	/// still count as tracking). `Ok(false)` when no such line exists
272	/// (bookmark isn't tracking this remote or doesn't exist). `Err` on
273	/// subprocess failure.
274	fn bm_track_one(repo: &Path, bookmark: &str, remote: &str) -> Result<bool, String> {
275	    let repo_str = repo.to_string_lossy();
276	    let output = std::process::Command::new("jj")
277	        .args(["bookmark", "list", "--tracked", bookmark, "-R", &repo_str])
278	        .output()
279	        .map_err(|e| format!("spawn: {e}"))?;
280	    if !output.status.success() {
281	        return Err(String::from_utf8_lossy(&output.stderr).trim().to_string());
282	    }
283	    let stdout = String::from_utf8_lossy(&output.stdout);
284	    // Accept both the synced form (`@origin:`) and the decorated
285	    // divergent form (`@origin (ahead by N commits):`), which still
286	    // represents a tracking relationship.
287	    let colon = format!("@{remote}:");
288	    let paren = format!("@{remote} ");
289	    Ok(stdout.lines().any(|l| {
290	        let t = l.trim_start();
291	        t.starts_with(&colon) || t.starts_with(&paren)
292	    }))
293	}
294	
295	fn main() -> ExitCode {
296	    CompleteEnv::with_factory(cli_with_banner).complete();
297	    let matches = cli_with_banner().get_matches();
298	    let cli = match Cli::from_arg_matches(&matches) {
299	        Ok(cli) => cli,
300	        Err(e) => {
301	            e.exit();
302	        }
303	    };
304	
305	    let log_path = cli.log.as_ref().map(|p| p.to_string_lossy().to_string());
306	    logging::CliLogger::init(cli.verbose, log_path.as_deref());
307	
308	    // The detached `finalize --exec` re-entry is the bot's
309	    // session-end child; it shouldn't print the banner (extra
310	    // chatter in its log) or surface user-facing failure markers
311	    // (those are meant for the user's next interactive run).
312	    let is_detached_exec = matches!(
313	        cli.command,
314	        Commands::Finalize(ref f) if f.exec
315	    );
316	
317	    // `-L` / `--no-label` on the read-only multi-repo subcommands
318	    // (chid, desc, list, show) makes their output script-parseable
319	    // — adding a leading `vc-x1 X.Y.Z` line would break that. Skip
320	    // the banner whenever the active subcommand has the flag set.
321	    let suppress_banner = match &cli.command {
322	        Commands::Chid(a) => a.common.no_label,
323	        Commands::Desc(a) => a.common.no_label,
324	        Commands::List(a) => a.common.no_label,
325	        Commands::Show(a) => a.common.no_label,
326	        _ => false,
327	    };
328	
329	    if !is_detached_exec {
330	        if !suppress_banner {
331	            // Banner on every normal run, mirroring what `--help`
332	            // shows at the top.
333	            log::info!("{BANNER}");
334	        }
335	        finalize::surface_previous_failures();
336	    }
337	
338	    // Command name for bm-track output (first positional arg after
339	    // the binary; clap has already validated it by the time we get here).
340	    let command_name = std::env::args().nth(1).unwrap_or_else(|| "?".to_string()); // OK: default when somehow invoked without a subcommand
341	
342	    if !is_detached_exec {
343	        bm_track("enter", &command_name);
344	    }
345	
346	    let exit_code = match cli.command {
347	        Commands::Chid(chid_args) => run_command(chid::chid(&chid_args)),
348	        Commands::Desc(desc_args) => run_command(desc::desc(&desc_args)),
349	        Commands::List(list_args) => run_command(list::list(&list_args)),
350	        Commands::Show(show_args) => run_command(show::show(&show_args)),
351	        Commands::ValidateDesc(validate_desc_args) => {
352	            run_command(validate_desc::validate_desc(&validate_desc_args))
353	        }
354	        Commands::FixDesc(fix_desc_args) => run_command(fix_desc::fix_desc(&fix_desc_args)),
355	        Commands::Clone(clone_args) => run_command(clone::clone_repo(&clone_args)),
356	        Commands::Init(init_args) => run_command(init::init(&init_args)),
357	        Commands::Symlink(symlink_args) => run_command(symlink::symlink(&symlink_args)),
358	        Commands::Sync(sync_args) => run_command(sync::sync(&sync_args)),
359	        Commands::Finalize(finalize_args) => {
360	            let opts = match finalize_args.into_opts(cli.log) {
361	                Ok(opts) => opts,
362	                Err(e) => {
363	                    error!("{e}");
364	                    return ExitCode::FAILURE;
365	                }
366	            };
367	            run_command(finalize::finalize(&opts))
368	        }
369	        Commands::Push(push_args) => run_command(push::push(&push_args)),
370	    };
371	
372	    if !is_detached_exec {

1344	### OF refactor (0.41.1-6.7)
1345	
1346	Sub-sub-step ladder (1)–(8) lifts every init OF into
1347	`src/options_flags/`, introduces the leaf / bundle / Pattern-A
1348	composition patterns, and locks the Flag/Option taxonomy by
1349	value-domain. Full per-substep edit lists live in commit
1350	bodies; this section captures the close-out shape, the
1351	sub-sub-step ToC, and durable decisions / watch-items.
1352	
1353	#### Close-out shape — separate commits, no mid-cycle squash
1354	
1355	Wink and the bot chose at close-out to **keep the 8
1356	sub-sub-step commits separate** rather than squash them into
1357	one `-6.7` commit (the prior pattern for `-6.0` through
1358	`-6.6`). Reasoning: the decomposition is itself an artifact —
1359	readers see leaf → bundle → naming progression; bisect lands
1360	on a specific stage; commit bodies double as design history.
1361	Squashing erases that.
1362	
1363	This sets a precedent: future steps may land as N commits when
1364	the decomposition is informative. The default for focused
1365	single-direction work is still one commit per step.
1366	
1367	The close-out commit at version `0.41.1-6.7` (no `-N` suffix)
1368	is itself a 9th commit holding the docs fold (substep-style →
1369	CLAUDE.md), in-flight pointer drop, this section, and todo.md
1370	flips. Total app-side: 9 commits for `-6.7`.
1371	
1372	#### Sub-sub-step ToC
1373	
1374	| commit | scope |
1375	|---|---|
1376	| `0.41.1-6.7-1` | cycle setup — Cargo bump + (1)–(7) ladder in todo.md |
1377	| `0.41.1-6.7-2` | `ConfigFlag` leaf + leaf/bundle/Pattern-A README; `FlagBundle` + `FlagParser` markers; init flattens `ConfigFlag` |
1378	| `0.41.1-6.7-3` | `DryRunFlag` + `PrivateFlag` leaves; init flatten |
1379	| `0.41.1-6.7-4` | `PushRetryFlags` leaf; `run_retry` takes `&PushRetryFlags`; multi-field-leaf-as-ref convention |
1380	| `0.41.1-6.7-5` | `UseTemplateFlag` + `AccountFlag` leaves; init flatten; substep-style.md authored |
1381	| `0.41.1-6.7-6` | `ScopeFlag` + `RepoFlag` leaves; `args.rs` retired |
1382	| `0.41.1-6.7-7` | `ProvisionCommon` bundle (DryRun + Private + PushRetry) — first OF bundle |
1383	| `0.41.1-6.7-8` | Flag/Option naming pass — three Bundle markers + two Parser contracts; rename 6 leaves `*Flag → *Option`; `ProvisionCommon → ProvisionOptionFlagBundle` |
1384	
1385	#### Naming watch — `ProvisionOptionFlagBundle`
1386	
1387	The first OF bundle landed as `ProvisionOptionFlagBundle` (25
1388	chars). Iteration considered `ProvisionCommon`,
1389	`ProvisionOptFlagBundle`, `ProvisionOptionFlag`,
1390	`ProvisionOFB`, and `ProvisionOptFlgBdle`. The full-word form
1391	won on consistency (matches `OptionParser`, `OptionBundle`,
1392	`*Option` leaves elsewhere), but Wink flagged at lock-in: "my
1393	guess we'll end up with something shorter in the future, just
1394	guessing."
1395	
1396	- **Why kept on the watch list:** the type appears in
1397	  `init.rs` imports, `test_helpers.rs` fixtures, and any
1398	  future provisioning subcommand (clone, etc.). Length will
1399	  be felt as more bundles land and more sites reference them.
1400	- **If a future cycle revisits naming:** the previously
1401	  rejected fallback was `ProvisionOptionFlag` (19 chars,
1402	  drops `Bundle` suffix; cue then lives in the file name and
1403	  marker impl, not the type name). Abbreviations
1404	  (`Bdle` / `Flg` / `OFB`) were explicitly rejected as
1405	  decoder-ring problems — don't re-propose them.
1406	
1407	
1408	
1409	- **Version + cycle line.** This work + the sync `--check`
1410	  fix land on the 0.41.x line. Init+clone = 0.41.1. Sync
1411	  fix = a separate cycle (likely 0.41.2). Then rebase the
1412	  in-flight 0.42.0 work on top of both.
1413	- **Path-prefix vocabulary.** `./NAME` and the standard
1414	  prefixes (`../`, `/`, `~/`, `~`), plus bare `.` and `..`
1415	  (POSIX cwd / parent — unambiguous). Bare alphanumeric
1416	  `NAME` is an error — explicit prefix required.
1417	- **`--private` on existing remote.** Warn and ignore;
1418	  visibility was set at create time.
1419	- **Cosmetic anomalies** from `notes/vc-x1-init.md` —
1420	  addressed at close-out, not deferred.
1421	- **`--scope=code` and `--scope=bot` for clone.** Dropped
1422	  from the menu. Manual decomposition (two `--scope=por`
1423	  clones + `vc-x1 symlink`) covers the use case.
```

720	## Testing
721	
722	The repo ships two flavors of tests:
723	
724	- **In-process tests** — `#[cfg(test)] mod tests { … }` blocks inside
725	  `src/*.rs`. These call library code directly (no subprocess spawn)
726	  and run fastest. The dual / POR fixture tests under
727	  `src/init.rs::tests` build throwaway workspaces by invoking
728	  `init::init_with_symlink` as a function.
729	- **CLI subprocess integration tests** — files under `tests/`. These
730	  spawn the `vc-x1` binary that Cargo built; its absolute path lives
731	  in the `CARGO_BIN_EXE_vc-x1` env var that Cargo sets at compile
732	  time, which `env!("CARGO_BIN_EXE_vc-x1")` reads and bakes into the
733	  test binary as a string literal. Argument parsing, exit codes, and
734	  stdout/stderr are all exercised end-to-end. Each subprocess gets a
```

> DEVELOPER

Not yet, I don't see why repo_utils.rs, sync.rs, cli_init.rs need to mention Fixture::new and init::init at all, thoughts?

> AGENT

Agree, the cross-references leak implementation detail.

- **`src/sync.rs:848`** — the module doc says fixtures are built "via `Fixture::new` (driving `init::init` in `--repo-local` mode)". The parenthetical is implementation detail the sync-test reader doesn't need; if it matters, it lives at `Fixture::new`.
- **`src/repo_utils.rs:224`** — the test comment justifies a contract on `prepare_local_repo`/`commit_initial` by pointing at the POR branch of `init`/`create_dual`. The contract reads independently ("helpers don't write config files; callers do") — the tour through `init.rs` is over-explanation.
- **`tests/cli_init.rs:5`** — the differentiator between the two test flavors is "in-process vs subprocess", not "calls init vs spawns binary". Naming the function adds nothing.
- **`README.md:728`** — borderline. Naming `init::init` does help a reader looking for the entry point, but the `create_symlink` aside is overhead.

Recommendation: drop the function-name refs in `sync.rs`, `repo_utils.rs`, and `cli_init.rs` (replace with the simpler "what the test is" framing), and leave `README.md` naming `init` since that's a "where to look" pointer for new readers — but trim the `create_symlink` aside out.

Trade-off: these were also useful as bisect breadcrumbs ("which test depends on init's contract") — but the symbol's still grep-able from the call site, so the breadcrumb cost is low.

Want me to apply that cleanup as part of `-6.8`, or as its own follow-up?

I see no reason to delay, thoughts?

> AGENT

Agree, no reason to delay — same sub-step. Applying the cleanup now.

Noted on the premature commit prep — holding on that.

On the rename: agreed in principle. `Fixture` is generic enough that the importer (`use crate::test_helpers::Fixture`) gets no signal about what it builds. `TestFixtureDual` / `TestFixturePor` self-document at every use site.

One nuance — the `Test` prefix is slightly redundant given the module is `test_helpers` and gated `#[cfg(test)]`. Variant worth weighing: `FixtureDual` / `FixturePor` (no `Test` prefix). Drops 4 chars at every call site. The `Dual` / `Por` suffix is what's actually carrying the meaning; `Test` is just reinforcement.

Recommendation: `FixtureDual` + `FixturePor`. Symmetric, and the `#[cfg(test)] mod test_helpers;` gate in main.rs already encodes the test-only role.

If you prefer `TestFixture*` for the explicit reinforcement, that's also fine — the trade-off is reinforcement vs. brevity, both defensible.

Either way it rides along in `-6.8` (related to the same doc-comment thread). Pick one and I'll apply.

> DEVELOPER

But in the small a developer looking at the call site, Fixture::new, doesn't know it's test only

> AGENT

Right, that lands it. The `#[cfg(test)]` gate is on the `mod test_helpers;` declaration, invisible at use. At a call site `Fixture::new(...)`, the reader can't tell test-only without chasing the import. The "redundancy" of the `Test` prefix isn't redundant where it matters — the use site is where the signal is missing today.

Going with `TestFixtureDual` / `TestFixturePor`. Want me to apply now?

I'm wonder if it's time to try to rebase and instead close out 0.41.1 now moving -6.9 and 0.41.1-7 to after the merge, thoughts?

> AGENT

Sensible — `-6.8` is a coherent stopping point. The init function shape is finalized, `-6.9` was already parked as "may not happen", and `-7` (Fixture migration + rename) is mechanical and doesn't depend on branch context.

Main tradeoff: the known CLAUDE.md conflict from the `notes/todo.md` rebase note has to be resolved. Doable, but real work — the conflict surface only grows the longer the branch sits, so doing it now is also the cheaper option.

Open questions before I'd commit to a plan:

1. **`-6` title scope.** `0.41.1-6` was titled "init refactor + symmetric .vc-config.toml schema". The symmetric-schema half — was that absorbed into `-6.4`/`-6.6` already, or is it still outstanding? If still outstanding, closing out now means dropping it from `-6` (acceptable, but worth naming explicitly).
2. **Order: rebase first vs close-out first?** I'd lean **close-out commit on the branch first** (clean `0.41.1` version marker as part of the chain), then rebase the whole chain onto main. Cleaner history that way.
3. **Where do `-6.9` and `-7` land post-merge?** Plain entries under `## Todo` (no version prefix until they get a cycle), or pre-allocate them as `0.41.2` / `0.41.3`?

If you confirm the symmetric-schema status and the order, I can lay out the close-out + rebase plan.

> DEVELOPER

Good catch, we've not done that, IIRC we're going add two new entries something like `code="/"` and bot="/.claude" were "/" means workspace root. There is a question which are you in?

> DEVELOPER

Good catch, we've not done that, IIRC we're going add two new entries something like `code="/"` and `bot="/.claude"` were "/" means workspace root. And you know which one you're in because you can know the name of the cwd and the last element will match `bot` if you're in bot otherwise you're in code. Thoughts?

> AGENT

Symmetric schema is a clean direction — same shape readable from either side, no per-role conditionals.

One concern with the cwd-basename heuristic: it only works when cwd **is** a role root. If cwd is `<root>/.claude/foo/`, the basename is `foo`, not `.claude`, so the rule says "you're in code" when you're actually in bot.

Robust answer: walk up to find the workspace root (the existing `find_workspace_root` already does this via `.vc-config.toml` with `path = "/"`), then check which configured path cwd falls under. Basename-match is a fast-path shortcut that works at exactly the role roots; below that you need the walk-up.

Bigger framing: this is real design work + implementation, not a close-out tidy-up. I'd suggest:

- **Drop the schema half from `-6`'s title at close-out** — rename it `init refactor` and explicitly note in the chores `-6` close-out that the symmetric-schema half deferred. Honest framing of what shipped.
- **Slot symmetric schema as its own cycle** — likely `0.41.2` or `0.42.x`. Give it room for the design call (basename heuristic vs walk-up vs both, what the migration path looks like for existing workspaces) rather than squeezing it under a close-out deadline.

That keeps the merge-now plan clean. Acceptable, or would you rather try to land at least a minimal symmetric schema before close-out?

1505 notes/chores-08.md
- **Composition over duplication.** `--scope=code,bot` is
  implemented as in-process composition of the
  `--scope=por` primitive — single source of truth for
  the actual clone/init operation, thin wrapper for the
  dual case.
- **Branch fork mechanics.** Code-side `init-clone-refactor`
  bookmark created at `6747a27`; bot-side partner bookmark
  at current `.claude` `main`. Main left alone as recovery
  anchor (code side). See
  [`forks-multi-user.md`](forks-multi-user.md) for the full
  discussion.

  **Correction at close-out (0.41.1-6.7):** the bot-side
  partner bookmark turned out to be unused — `.claude` is a
  linear journal that only needs `main`, and all 8
  sub-sub-step `.claude` commits + the close-out landed on
  `.claude main` regardless of the partner bookmark's
  position. The partner bookmark misled the bot during
  close-out push (it pushed app-side `main` instead of
  `init-clone-refactor` because the convention that "the
  bookmark to push is `main`" was carried over from
  single-branch projects). The convention is now codified in
  `CLAUDE.md > ## Sub-step Workflow > .claude cadence`:
  `.claude` does not maintain partner bookmarks for app-side
  branches. App-side keeps feature branches; `.claude` stays
  on `main`.

# References

[1]: forks-multi-user.md
```

> AGENT

Two ways to read this:

**Convention precedent says no.** Looking at prior cycles (0.37.0, 0.38.0, 0.40.0, 0.41.0), `## Done` and `done.md` both follow one-line-per-top-level-step. Sub-substep detail (`-6.0` … `-6.8`) was never archived in those files. The detail lives in chores-08.md (each sub-step has a design subsection) and in the commit log.

**But it's a reasonable instinct.** 0.41.1 is the first cycle deep enough to have sub-sub-step ladders (`-6.M`, `-6.7-K`). The 8-line `## Done` summary I added flattens that decomposition — readable, but loses the leaf-level granularity the in-progress view carried.

Three options if you want to preserve more:
- **(a) Expand `## Done`** — list `-6.0` through `-6.8` as separate Done lines (8 extra lines). Matches the 0.37.0 / 0.38.0 multi-step shape but for sub-substeps.
- **(b) Archive the deleted ladder verbatim to `done.md`** as a "0.41.1 detail block". Preserves the exact structure (including `-6.7-1` … `-6.7-8` if you go to that depth). Sets a new precedent for deep ladders.
- **(c) Leave as-is.** Trust chores-08.md + git log to carry the detail.

My lean: **(c)**. chores-08.md already does this job, and `done.md` repeating the same content adds bulk without new information. But if discoverability matters (someone scanning `done.md` for "what shipped in 0.41.1-6.5"), (b) is the right move.

Your call — happy to apply (a) or (b) before pushing.

**Squash:** my "no broader squash" line was premature — I was thinking "this close-out commit doesn't squash earlier work" rather than addressing the cycle-level squash decision the workflow asks for. Per CLAUDE.md > "Cycle close-out — squash or keep separate?", default is squash, deviate when decomposition is informative.

The branch carries ~30 commits — top-level `-0` through `-6` (some with their own sub-substep ladders), the 9-commit `-6.7` decomposition, `-6.8`, and this close-out. Three plausible schemes:

1. **No squash** — all ~30 commits land on main. Most history preserved; noisiest log.
2. **Single squash** — entire 0.41.1 collapses to one "init + clone redesign" commit. Cleanest log; loses the sub-step structure entirely.
3. **Selective squash** — squash within each sub-step cycle's leaves (e.g., `-6.5`'s 5 sub-substeps → 1, `-6.6`'s 3 → 1), but keep top-level `-0`…`-6` plus `-6.7`'s informative 9-commit decomposition separate. Preserves the design-progression artifact, drops mechanical churn.

I don't remember the prior discussion — you mentioned we've talked about it. Which scheme did we land on, or is the call open?

> DEVELOPER

We've just talked about squashing as it might make the merge easier, I have a feeling the merge is going to be ugly

> AGENT

Worth interrogating the assumption — squashing might not help the merge math the way it sounds like it should.

When `main` rebases onto `init-clone-refactor`, jj replays each `main` commit one-by-one onto the new base. The conflict surface per replay is "this `main` commit's patch vs the cumulative diff `init-clone-refactor` brought in" — which is the same whether `init-clone-refactor` is 30 commits or 1 squashed commit. Squashing `init-clone-refactor` cleans up the *final log on main* but doesn't reduce the number of conflict resolutions.

What would actually reduce conflict resolutions:
- **Squashing `main`** before rebase — fewer replay operations, fewer chances to hit a conflict. Costs 0.42.0's history.
- **Merge commit instead of rebase** — single conflict surface at the merge commit, both branches' history preserved.

Recommendation: before deciding squash strategy, look at the actual conflict surface. `jj diff --from main --to init-clone-refactor --stat` and the reverse will tell us which files both branches touched. Then we know whether the merge is genuinely ugly (lots of mutual file overlap) or just feels intimidating (mostly disjoint, with the known CLAUDE.md hotspot).

Want me to run those diagnostics now, before touching the close-out?

# init-clone-refactor: recovery post-mortem + playbook

Drafted 2026-05-01 as a forward-looking brief while the
branch was still broken; rewritten 2026-05-02 after the
recovery completed. Documents what went wrong, the
playbook that fixed it, and the strategic decisions
still open.

## Status as of 2026-05-02 (post-recovery)

- `init-clone-refactor` bookmark is at `a8c1eefe`
  (chid `sumruqqomnzs`) — the published clean
  `0.41.1-6.5` commit.
- All 13 commits from fork point `ykxrlkkwqpyv`
  (`0.41.0`, cid `6747a27b`) through `0.41.1-6.5` are
  unconflicted. `cargo test --bins` passes 331/331 on
  the recovered tip.
- Recovery via `jj bookmark set init-clone-refactor
  -r init-clone-refactor@origin --allow-backwards
  -R .`. The published remote branch turned out to
  be the canonical-good version; local was holding a
  destructively-rewritten copy.
- 8 broken local-only chids still visible as orphans
  in `jj log` (no bookmark, GC-eligible). `jj abandon`
  cleanup is cosmetic, deferrable.

## Status as of 2026-05-01 (pre-recovery, historical)

- Branch existed locally with 8 commits all in
  conflict state: `0.41.1-0` (chore: open) → unnamed
  → `0.41.1-5` (init reshape) → `0.41.1-6.0..6.5`
  (POR baseline tests then progressive split of
  `create_local_repo`). Conflicts on the same files
  all the way down: `Cargo.toml`, `Cargo.lock`,
  `notes/todo.md`, `notes/chores-08.md` (3-sided),
  `src/config.rs`, `src/init.rs`, `src/clone.rs`.
- Original brief's hypothesis — "interrupted rebase
  or substep-style rewrite" — turned out to be wrong.
  See "Root cause" below.
- `@origin (ahead by at least 10 commits, behind by
  8 commits)` was what jj reported. The "ahead by
  *at least* 10" caveat (vs the precise "behind by
  8") was the tell that the remote held more commits
  than local — i.e. local had *lost* work, not just
  rewritten it. Worth noticing earlier next time.

## Why this matters (intent)

Refactor `init` and `clone` to share `init_one` /
`clone_one` primitives plus orchestrators
(`init_dual` / `clone_dual`). Eliminates duplicated
lifecycle logic between init and clone, and gives a
single chokepoint for `--scope`-related changes —
important because every subcommand picking up
`--scope` lands on the same `Scope` shape, and
reusable foundations mean those changes happen in
one place rather than parallel implementations.
Design captured in `notes/chores-07.md > ## init +
clone redesign (0.41.1)`.

## Root cause (post-mortem)

The bot thinks the local rewrites came from a
destructive `jj squash` (or equivalent operation)
that collapsed published commits `0.41.1-1`, `-2`,
`-3` into `-4`. Symptoms supporting this:

- Three chids existed on the remote (`mkwqykksykoo`
  `-1`, `plxsuynrtwks` `-2`, `xtrworrlkozn` `-3`)
  with no local counterpart at all.
- `uuzwzxzkszwq` (the local "unnamed" commit) shared
  the *same chid* as the remote `0.41.1-4` commit,
  but had its description stripped — consistent with
  a squash that pulled three commits' content into
  `-4`'s tree but lost the descriptions.
- Conflicts cascaded down `-5` and `-6.0..6.5`
  because those commits' parent tree no longer
  matched what they had been authored against — the
  rewritten `-4` diverged from what the descendants
  expected.

Net effect: 3 commits of real work disappeared from
local, `-4`'s description was lost, and every
descendant inherited a conflict against the changed
parent tree. The remote was untouched and held the
canonical-good version of all of it.

- vice versa (force-rewrite main — only if there's
  a reason that justifies rewriting published
  history),

## Recovery playbook (used 2026-05-02)

Reusable for similar "local rewrites broke a branch
that the remote holds in clean form" situations.
All read-only steps come first; mutation only after
verification.

1. **Back up first.** Cheap insurance.
   - `cp -a . ../<dir>-<date>-N` — full snapshot,
     includes `.jj/op` history. Or `rsync -a
     --exclude=target/ ./ ../<dir>/` if `target/`
     bloat matters.
   - `git clone <remote> ../<dir>-clone-<date>-N`
     — canonical published state, jj-free, browsable
     in gitk without local-rewrite confusion.
   - `jj op log -R . > ../<dir>-op-log-<date>-N.txt`
     — text-readable op log, independent of jj.

2. **Verify remote tip is unconflicted (read-only):**
   ```
   jj git fetch -R .
   jj log -R . -r '<fork-chid>::<branch>@origin' \
     -T '... if(conflict, "CONFLICT", "ok") ...'
   ```
   Every line must read `[ok]`. If any are
   `[CONFLICT]`, the remote isn't a safe target and
   this playbook doesn't apply.

3. **Verify local-only commits are all chid-rewrites
   of remote commits (read-only):**
   ```
   jj log -R . -r '<branch>@origin..<branch>' \
     -T 'change_id.short() ++ ...'
   ```
   Every chid here must also appear on the remote.
   If any chid is unique to local, the reset would
   lose work — investigate before proceeding.

4. **Reset bookmark to remote:**
   ```
   jj bookmark set <branch> -r <branch>@origin \
     --allow-backwards -R .
   ```
   The `--allow-backwards` is required because jj
   guards against accidental backwards moves.

5. **Sanity test:**
   ```
   jj new <branch> -R .
   cargo test --bins      # or full cargo clean ; cargo test for paranoia
   ```

6. **Optional cleanup:** `jj abandon` the orphan
   local-only chids if you don't want them in
   `jj log`. Cosmetic; jj's GC eventually prunes
   them anyway.

## Diagnostic technique (reusable)

Two questions to ask when a branch is mysteriously
conflicted:

- **Are the same chids on the remote in clean form?**
  `jj log -r '<fork>::<branch>@origin'` with the
  conflict-status template tells you whether the
  remote has a working version of every commit.
- **Is local missing any chids the remote has?**
  Compare chid lists between
  `<fork>::<branch>@origin` and
  `<fork>::<branch>` — gaps indicate commits the
  local branch *lost* (squash, abandon, or similar).

If both yield "yes, remote clean + local missing
chids," the reset playbook above is the shortest
path. If only the first yields yes (no missing
chids, local just rewrote into broken state), you
can shrink step 1 of the playbook (work-loss risk
is gone) and just reset.

The fork point itself can be derived without
hand-scanning parent columns:
```
jj log -r 'heads(::<branch-A> & ::<branch-B>)'
```
Returns the latest commit that's an ancestor of
both — the divergence point.

## JJ-CONFLICT-README (the thing that shows in gitk)

When jj has a conflicted commit, it serializes the
conflict state into a git-side tree containing:

- `JJ-CONFLICT-README` — text file explaining the
  format.
- `.jjconflict-base-N/` — the base side(s) of the
  three-way merge.
- `.jjconflict-side-N/` — each conflict input.

Plain git tools (gitk, GitHub UI, etc.) display
this as real files. **Recoverable** — `jj` itself
knows the commit is conflicted and `jj resolve`
walks the inputs. Don't `git checkout` a conflicted
commit and start editing files directly; jj's
invariants get confused. The README itself includes
the recovery hint (`jj abandon` if you accidentally
end up in this state).

> AGENT

Sound instinct. Two ways to achieve "leave main and ICR alone" in jj, with different ceremony levels:

**Option A — snapshot-and-rebase (simpler, more idiomatic jj):**
```
jj bookmark create main-pre-rebase -r main      # snapshot
jj rebase -b main -d init-clone-refactor        # main moves to new tip
```
main ends up at the rebased tip; `main-pre-rebase` preserves the original location. Recovery is `jj bookmark set main -r main-pre-rebase` if we don't like the result.

**Option B — duplicate-then-rebase (closer to your stated mental model — "main literally doesn't move"):**
```
jj duplicate -r 'init-clone-refactor..main'                # copies main's 7 commits
jj bookmark create rebase-main-onto-icr-1 -r <new-tip>     # name the duplicate chain's tip
jj rebase -b rebase-main-onto-icr-1 -d init-clone-refactor # rebase the duplicates only
```
main and init-clone-refactor stay literally untouched. The new bookmark holds the rebase product.

Worth noting: **`main@origin` is unchanged either way until we force-push**. The "leave main alone" question is purely about local bookmark position — origin's main is safe regardless. So both options are equally safe; the choice is about local mental model.

Recommendation: **Option A**. Less ceremony, same recoverability via the snapshot. Option B is right if you want to compare two rebase strategies side-by-side (`-1`, `-2`, …) or if having `main` literally not move matters for tooling/eyes.

Either way the rebase operation itself is the same — Option B just adds a duplicate step in front.

Move revisions to different parent(s)

This command moves revisions to different parent(s) while preserving the
changes (diff) in the revisions.

There are three different ways of specifying which revisions to rebase:

* `--source/-s` to rebase a revision and its descendants
* `--branch/-b` to rebase a whole branch, relative to the destination
* `--revisions/-r` to rebase the specified revisions without their
  descendants

If no option is specified, it defaults to `-b @`.

There are three different ways of specifying where the revisions should be
rebased to:

* `--onto/-o` to rebase the revisions onto the specified targets
* `--insert-after/-A` to rebase the revisions onto the specified targets and
  to rebase the targets' descendants onto the rebased revisions
* `--insert-before/-B` to rebase the revisions onto the specified targets'
  parents and to rebase the targets and their descendants onto the rebased
  revisions

See the sections below for details about the different ways of specifying
which revisions to rebase where.

If a working-copy revision gets abandoned, it will be given a new, empty
revision. This is true in general; it is not specific to this command.

### Specifying which revisions to rebase

With `--source/-s`, the command rebases the specified revision and its
descendants to the destination. For example, `jj rebase -s M -o O` would
transform your history like this (letters followed by an apostrophe are
post-rebase versions):

```text
O           N'
|           |
| N         M'
| |         |
| M         O
| |    =>   |
| | L       | L
| |/        | |
| K         | K
|/          |/
J           J
```

Each revision passed to `-s` will become a direct child of the destination,
so if you instead run `jj rebase -s M -s N -o O` (or `jj rebase -s 'M|N' -o
O`) in the example above, then N' would instead be a direct child of O.

With `--branch/-b`, the command rebases the whole "branch" containing the
specified revision. A "branch" is the set of revisions that includes:

* the specified revision and ancestors that are not also ancestors of the
  destination
```

Expected — jj's default immutable set covers commits reachable from `trunk()` (i.e., `main@origin`), which is exactly the 7 main commits we're trying to rewrite. The guard exists precisely to prevent accidental rewrites of published history.

Per CLAUDE.md this is the kind of flag that needs explicit acknowledgment of what it bypasses — and that's exactly what we're doing: we know main's commits are published, we have the backup, the force-push is a planned later step.

Current state confirmed:
- `@` = `wuszqzmplozx 53950d04` (empty working copy on top of `init-clone-refactor`)
- `init-clone-refactor` = `slvprlpwpyoq 6c1302fb` (the close-out)

**Answer to your question — neither "altered" nor "copy", it's "rewritten in place":**

jj's `rebase` doesn't create copies. It rewrites the source commits — the **chid `ozvrpkrx` is preserved** (jj's logical change identity), but the **commit ID changes** (because the parent changes, the resulting commit object has a different SHA). The original commit ID `1bb61bb7` becomes orphaned (no bookmark, no working-copy reference, GC-eligible).

**Destination is `init-clone-refactor`, not `@`.** The 7 main commits will land on top of `slvprlpwpyoq` (`init-clone-refactor`'s tip), not on `wuszqzmplozx` (`@`). `@` is on init-clone-refactor's side of the divergence — it's not in main's branch — so it's not part of the rebase set and stays where it is.

**Concern I want to flag — the `main-pre-rebase` snapshot may not work as expected.** In jj, when a commit is rewritten, **all** bookmarks pointing to it follow to the rewritten version. Since `main` and `main-pre-rebase` both point to commit `uxymmmynrrqs`, the rewrite of that commit may pull both bookmarks forward. If so, the snapshot doesn't actually preserve the old location — `jj op restore` becomes the real recovery mechanism, and the bookmark is decorative.

Want me to verify this empirically (a tiny test) or via doc lookup before running the rebase, so we know whether the snapshot is doing what we wanted?

> AGENT

Precise answer:

**`uxymmmyn` (the chid) does NOT change.** jj's chid is the "logical change" identity and is designed to persist across rewrites. So after the rebase, there will still be a commit with chid `uxymmmyn`.

**The commit ID at `uxymmmyn` does change.** Currently `00739393`; after rebase, a new SHA — because the parent chain is different. The original commit ID `00739393` becomes orphaned (no chid, no bookmark pointing to it; recoverable via `jj op restore`).

**`slvprlpw` (icr) does not change at all** — both chid and commit ID stay the same. It's the destination, not part of the source set being rewritten.

**Implication for the snapshot.** This confirms the concern I flagged: bookmarks track chid, not commit ID. Since `main` and `main-pre-rebase` both point to chid `uxymmmyn`, when that chid's commit ID is rewritten, both bookmarks follow to the new commit ID. The snapshot bookmark **doesn't preserve the original commit location** — `jj op restore` is the actual recovery mechanism. To truly preserve the original commits, we'd need `jj duplicate` (creates new chids), not just a snapshot bookmark.

Recovery options going in, then:
- **`jj op restore`** — undoes the rebase operation entirely (this is the real safety net).
- **`jj git fetch` + `jj bookmark set main -r main@origin --allow-backwards`** — pulls main from origin, where the canonical pre-rebase version still lives until we force-push.

Want me to delete `main-pre-rebase` (it's not doing the job we wanted) and rely on `jj op restore` / origin for recovery? Or leave it for the descriptive value (the bookmark name is self-documenting even if it auto-follows)?

So it's a destructive change to the contents of uxymmmyn and it's sha1 (cid) is going to change, and what happens to uxymmmyn decsendants are they orphaned or ???

> AGENT

Two separate things in your question. Let me unpack:

**On "destructive":**
- The chid `uxymmmyn` persists — the *logical change* is preserved.
- The commit object at SHA `00739393` is abandoned (no chid, no bookmark, GC-eligible).
- A new commit object exists with a fresh SHA carrying the same chid `uxymmmyn`.
- The **diff/patch** of `uxymmmyn` is preserved (jj reapplies the same change to the new base). Only the resulting *tree* differs because the base is different — and if there's a conflict, the new commit is marked conflicted.

So "destructive" in the sense that the original SHA is orphaned: yes. "Destructive" in the sense that the change content is lost: no — preserved as the chid + diff.

**On descendants — in our specific case, `uxymmmyn` has none.** Looking at the topology I read earlier:

```
wuszqzmp  @ (empty WC, child of init-clone-refactor)
slvprlpw  init-clone-refactor
uxymmmyn  main, main-pre-rebase  ← main's tip, no children
```

`@` (`wuszqzmp`) descends from `init-clone-refactor`, not from `main`. So `uxymmmyn` is a leaf and has no descendants to worry about.

**In general (for future awareness):** if `uxymmmyn` had descendants, jj would cascade-rewrite them. Each descendant gets a new commit ID (because its parent's commit ID changed) but keeps its chid. They're not orphaned — they follow the rewrite. Bookmarks pointing at them follow too.

**Net for our rebase:** the 7 main commits each get rewritten in place (chids preserved, commit IDs change). No descendants beyond main's tip. `init-clone-refactor` and `@` are untouched.

> AGENT

Good news — `jj duplicate` with `-o`/`--onto` does duplicate-and-rebase as one shot, leaving the original commits literally untouched. We don't even need `--ignore-immutable` because the originals aren't being rewritten — we're creating new commits.

One small precision on your mental model: in jj, duplicates get **new chids AND new commit IDs** (the chid is encoded in commit metadata, so cid changes when chid changes — they always travel together). What's preserved is the **tree** (file contents) and **description**. So the Y structure works, but it's "new identity, same content," not "new chid, same cid."

**Proposed command:**

```
jj duplicate -r 'init-clone-refactor..main' -o init-clone-refactor -R .
```

**What it does:**
- Source revset `init-clone-refactor..main` = the 7 main commits (commits reachable from `main` but not from `init-clone-refactor`).
- Destination `init-clone-refactor` = `slvprlpw` (icr's tip).
- jj duplicates each of the 7 commits, chained: root (`ozvrpkrx`) becomes a child of `slvprlpw`; the rest chain off the duplicates in order.
- Original `main`, `init-clone-refactor`, the 7 main commits — all untouched.
- 7 new commits exist with new chids/cids, on top of icr.
- Conflicts (same surface as rebase would have) surface as conflicted commits in the duplicate chain.

After this lands, we bookmark the new tip as `rebase-main-onto-icr-1` and have the Y structure: original `main` on one branch, duplicate chain on the other.

Approve?

> DEVELOPER

what would `jj duplicate -r 'init-clone-refactor..main' -o ykxrlkkw` where ykxrlkkw is the common ancestor?

> AGENT

Cleaner separation — that's actually the better shape. Let me think through what would happen:

**Source revset `init-clone-refactor..main`** = the 7 main commits (root: `ozvrpkrx`).

**Destination `ykxrlkkw`** = the common ancestor, which is **already the parent** of `ozvrpkrx` in the original chain.

**Effect:**
- jj duplicates root `ozvrpkrx` onto `ykxrlkkw` — producing `ozvrpkrx'` as a sibling of the original `ozvrpkrx` (both children of `ykxrlkkw`).
- Other commits duplicate onto the new duplicates: `ttzwvpoq'` is a child of `ozvrpkrx'`, etc.
- Result: a parallel chain of 7 commits rooted at `ykxrlkkw`, mirroring main's chain exactly.
- **No conflicts.** Each duplicate has the same parent as its original, applies the same diff → produces the same tree. Pure clone.
- main, icr, original 7 commits — all untouched.

**This gives you a true Y topology** at `ykxrlkkw`: original main on one prong, duplicate on the other. The duplicate is a pristine local copy; the rebase onto icr happens as a separate second step on the duplicate prong only.

**Workflow becomes 2 clean steps:**
1. `jj duplicate -r 'init-clone-refactor..main' -o ykxrlkkw -R .` — pure clone, no conflicts, low-risk.
2. `jj rebase -b <new-tip-bookmark> -o init-clone-refactor -R . --ignore-immutable` — the actual rebase, where conflicts surface, on the duplicate only.

If step 2 produces something we don't like, the duplicate sits there as a parallel preserved chain we can throw away and retry — main never touched.

`--ignore-immutable` not needed for step 1 (no commits being rewritten, just new ones created with `ykxrlkkw` as parent). Step 2 still needs it because the duplicates inherit the immutable-via-trunk status from their `ykxrlkkw` ancestor.

**After step 2 (rebase the duplicate onto icr) it collapses back to two fingers:**

```
                          main (ykxrlkkw → 7 main commits → uxymmmyn)
                         /
ykxrlkkw ───────────────●
                         \
                          icr (ykxrlkkw → 23 → slvprlpw → 7 rebased dups → uxymmmyn'')
```

The duplicate chain that lived at `ykxrlkkw` after step 1 gets rewritten in step 2 — its chids persist (`ozvrpkrx'`, etc.) but the cids change because the parent chain is now `slvprlpw`-rooted instead of `ykxrlkkw`-rooted. Original duplicate cids at `ykxrlkkw` become orphaned.

**What's "preserved" through the whole sequence:** `main` itself, all the way through. The duplicate is the *sacrifice* — the thing we mutate so main doesn't have to be touched. If step 2 produces a mess, we throw away the rebased duplicate (and the now-orphan original duplicate goes with it via op-log/GC) and try again — main is never disturbed.

Want me to run step 1 now?

```
jj duplicate -r 'init-clone-refactor..main' -o ykxrlkkw -R .
```

Abandon a revision

Abandon a revision, rebasing descendants onto its parent(s). The behavior is similar to `jj restore
--changes-in`; the difference is that `jj abandon` gives you a new change, while `jj restore`
updates the existing change.

If a working-copy commit gets abandoned, it will be given a new, empty commit. This is true in
general; it is not specific to this command.

Usage: jj abandon [OPTIONS] [REVSETS]...

Arguments:
  [REVSETS]...
          The revision(s) to abandon (default: @) [aliases: -r]

Options:
      --retain-bookmarks
          Do not delete bookmarks pointing to the revisions to abandon
          
          Bookmarks will be moved to the parent revisions instead.

      --restore-descendants
          Do not modify the content of the children of the abandoned commits

  -h, --help
```

> AGENT

`jj abandon` takes multiple revsets. Both orphans are heads (no descendants, since they came out of `all() ~ ::(bookmarks() | @)`), so abandon won't trigger any rebase cascade — it just makes them unreachable in jj log. Recoverable via `jj op restore` if anything goes wrong.

Note: the `tk` orphan (0.32.0-era) is wholly unrelated to the current rebase work — it's been sitting there for a while. Up to you whether to clean it up now (since we're already touching jj orphans) or leave it for a separate sweep.

**Two options:**

**A) Clean up both orphans now:**
```
jj abandon 4ee7f937 f2699a29 -R .
```

**B) Just kill the divergent one (the one that prompted the question):**
```
jj abandon 4ee7f937 -R .
```

**C) Skip cleanup, proceed with duplicate.** The orphans don't block the rebase; cleanup is purely cosmetic.

Which?

> DEVELOPER

Can we undo with `jj op revert` or maybe restore, not sure the difference, from `jj op -h`:
  restore    Create a new operation that restores the repo to an earlier state
  revert     Create a new operation that reverts an earlier operation

> AGENT

Good distinction to nail down — they're different tools.

**`jj op restore <op>`** — sets the repo state back to exactly what it was at operation `<op>`. Like loading a snapshot. Any operations after `<op>` are effectively erased from the active state (still in op log, still recoverable).

**`jj op revert <op>`** — creates the inverse of operation `<op>` and applies it. Like git revert — undoes one specific operation while keeping anything that happened after it.

**Concretely:**

If I do these in order — abandon orphan → duplicate main → rebase — then realize the **abandon** was wrong:
- `jj op restore <pre-abandon>` undoes the abandon AND the duplicate AND the rebase. Everything after the abandon is rolled back.
- `jj op revert <abandon>` un-abandons the orphan (brings it back) while keeping the duplicate and the rebase. Targeted undo.

For just undoing an abandon with nothing else happening after, both work — `restore <pre-abandon>` and `revert <abandon>` produce the same result. The difference matters when there's intervening work.

Either way, `jj op log` is how you find the op ID to pass:

```
jj op log -R . | head
```

So yes, the abandon is reversible by either path. Want to proceed with one of A/B/C? My inclination would be **A** (clean up both, `jj op restore` is the safety net for both at once if needed) but **C** (skip) is also fine — orphans don't actually block the rebase.

And this isn't:
wink@3900x 26-05-06T01:22:23.085Z:~/data/prgs/rust/vc-x1 (init-clone-refactor+1)

123
wink@3900x 26-05-06T01:23:50.503Z:~/data/prgs/rust/vc-x1 (init-clone-refactor+1)

> AGENT

The two operators answer different questions:

**`tkklqwor::main` (DAG range, inclusive)** — "commits that are BOTH descendants of `tkklqwor` AND ancestors of `main`." Empty here because `tkklqwor` is **not an ancestor of `main`**. They're on parallel branches that diverged way back. No commit is on a path going through both.

**`tkklqwor..main` (range, exclusive)** — "ancestors of `main` that are NOT ancestors of `tkklqwor`." This returns everything in main's lineage minus the shared prefix back to their actual GCA (somewhere before 0.32.0). 123 lines = lots of commits (main's whole history minus the bit it shares with the 0.32.0-draft branch's prefix).

**Quick rule for jj revsets:**

| Operator | Meaning | Symmetric? | Empty when… |
|---|---|---|---|
| `X::Y` | Path from X to Y on DAG (inclusive) | No — requires X reachable from Y | X is not an ancestor of Y |
| `X..Y` | Ancestors of Y minus ancestors of X | No | Y is an ancestor of X (or equal) |
| `X..` | Descendants of X (exclusive) | — | X has no descendants |
| `::Y` | All ancestors of Y (inclusive) | — | (never; always at least Y) |

The empty `::` result is actually the **strongest evidence** that `tkklqwor` (the 0.32.0 draft) is unrelated to main's current line — confirming the abandon decision is safe (and confirming why it's already published as `v0.32.0-draft@origin` rather than landed on main).

> AGENT

These two queries answer different questions. The interesting finding from the second one: the orphan is also `(conflict)` — meaning it has unresolved conflict markers in its tree. Confirms the post-mortem's diagnosis (destructive squash left a broken commit).

**`4ee7f937..`** — when you omit the right-hand side, jj defaults it to `visible_heads()`. So this means **"ancestors of any visible head, minus ancestors of 4ee7f937."** Since 4ee7f937 has very few ancestors (just `ykxrlkkw` and the small history before that), almost all visible history qualifies — main's chain, icr's chain, the v0.32.0-draft chain, the working copy. That's why it's huge.

**`4ee7f937::`** — when you omit the right-hand side, this means **"descendants of 4ee7f937, inclusive of 4ee7f937 itself."** The orphan has no descendants (no bookmark, nothing built on it), so the result is just 4ee7f937 alone.

**Key insight on the asymmetry:** the two operators are NOT mirror images:

| Form | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `..X` | Ancestors of X (exclusive of X's parents… well, of nothing — same as `::X`?) |
| `X..` | `X..visible_heads()` — everything reachable not in X's history |
| `::X` | Ancestors of X (inclusive) |
| `X::` | Descendants of X (inclusive) |

So `..` defaults its right-hand side to `visible_heads()` (a *broad* set — all heads), while `::` defaults to nothing on the right, meaning "just descendants." The defaults are different, which is why the same input commit produces wildly different results.

**Bottom line for our cleanup:** `4ee7f937` is conflict-state, divergent, no descendants, no remote refs, no bookmarks. Textbook safe-to-abandon. Want me to run:

```
jj abandon 4ee7f937 -R .
```

> AGENT

Yes, exactly correct. Crisp summary:

- **`X::Y`** — both endpoints **included**. The DAG path from X to Y, walking forward.
- **`X..Y`** — left endpoint (X) **excluded**, right endpoint (Y) **included**. Equivalent to `::Y ~ ::X` — "ancestors of Y, minus ancestors of X (X itself counts as one of its own ancestors)."

The asymmetry of `..` is what makes it useful for "what's *new* on Y since branching from X" — the divergence point itself is shared so you usually want to exclude it.

Mnemonic that helps me: **`..` is "between, not including the floor"** (range, exclusive on left). **`::` is "including the floor"** (DAG range, inclusive both sides).

So for our planned duplicate command, `init-clone-refactor..main` gives us **exactly the 7 main commits** — none of init-clone-refactor's commits, none of the GCA. That's the right source set.

Ready to proceed with step 1?

```
jj duplicate -r 'init-clone-refactor..main' -o gca-icr-main -R .
```

hmmmmmmm, I'm thinking we should rebase using `main`, that way the ochid are still valid, main-2 is just a backup in case the shit hits the fan. But actually if it does we'd probably either clone the repo again or use the copy, ../vc-x1-20260505-1, correct?

> AGENT

You're right on all counts. Let me think through it:

**Ochids — your reasoning is correct.** Rebase preserves chids; ochid trailers reference chids, not cids. So:
- main's ochid trailers (text in descriptions) point at `.claude` chids → stay valid.
- `.claude`'s ochid trailers point at main's chids → those chids are preserved, still valid.

Both directions intact.

If we instead promoted `main-2` to be the new main, `.claude`'s ochid trailers would point at the **original** chids (`uxymmmyn`, etc.) which would be orphaned, while main-2's chids (`wrpuqtrq`, etc.) would have no `.claude` referrers. Bidirectional break. So rebasing main directly is the right call.

**Recovery hierarchy if shit hits fan:**
1. **`jj op restore`** — local op-log undo. First line.
2. **`jj git fetch` + reset main from origin** — origin still has canonical pre-rebase main until we force-push. Second line.
3. **`../vc-x1-20260505-1` filesystem copy** — heavy hammer; restores everything including `.claude` state. Third line.
4. **Re-clone** — nuclear option.

`main-2` sits between (1) and (3) — local-jj-level snapshot with different chids, won't auto-follow when main is rewritten. It's cheap to keep; might be useful for `jj diff main..main-2` during conflict resolution to verify the rebase produced what we expected.

**Side observation worth flagging — `main-pre-rebase` is actually useless.** It points to the same chid as main (`uxymmmyn`). When main is rewritten, both bookmarks follow because bookmarks track chid. So `main-pre-rebase` will NOT preserve the original location. We can leave it (no harm) or delete it (clarity). I noted this concern before the rebase started but didn't act on it — worth deleting now to avoid confusion.

**Two cleanups to consider before rebasing:**

```
jj bookmark delete main-pre-rebase -R .
```

Then the rebase:

```
jj rebase -b main -o init-clone-refactor -R . --ignore-immutable
```

Or skip the cleanup and just rebase. Your call on `main-pre-rebase`.

> DEVELOPER

Actually, this is going to "fail" with conflict(s). Will rebase stop at the first conflict?

> AGENT

No — jj's design is fundamentally different from git here. **jj rebase doesn't stop on conflict.** It completes the whole rebase, producing **conflicted commits** for any rebases where auto-merge fails.

This is a deliberate jj design choice: conflicts are stored *as data in the commit's tree* (jj's own conflict representation, not git-style markers). A conflicted commit is a first-class artifact you can continue to work with — `jj log` shows it with `(conflict)`, and `jj resolve` walks the conflict inputs.

So after the rebase completes, we expect to see something like:

```
×  uxymmmyn ... (conflict)  docs: init-clone-refactor recovery WIP
×  kyurxpnu ... (conflict)  docs: substep protocol + jj revsets
×  lulqxovr ... (conflict)  docs: capture 0.41.1 init+clone redesign plan
×  plyvnpxt ... (conflict)  refactor: sync --scope retrofit
×  mvusyowm ... (conflict)  feat: --scope parser + init retrofit
×  ttzwvpoq ... (conflict)  refactor: Scope enum
×  ozvrpkrx ... (conflict)  chore: open 0.42.0 cycle
○  slvprlpw  init-clone-refactor  chore: close-out 0.41.1
```

Important consequence: **conflicts cascade.** If commit N conflicts, commit N+1 inherits those conflicts (it's applying its diff to a conflicted tree) and adds its own. So we might see all 7 commits marked conflicted, even though the *unique* conflicts at each level are smaller.

**Post-rebase workflow — resolve from the bottom up:**

1. `jj log` to see the chain and `(conflict)` markers.
2. `jj resolve --list -r <oldest-conflicted>` to see files needing resolution at that commit.
3. Resolve via working-copy edits or `jj resolve` (launches merge tool).
4. As soon as commit N is resolved, jj automatically rebases N's descendants onto the resolved version. If N's resolution incidentally fixes downstream conflicts, those commits clear too.
5. Walk up to the next still-conflicted commit, repeat.

So jj resolves are propagating, not isolated — fixing the bottom often clears more than just that commit.

**Recovery if conflicts get unmanageable:** `jj op restore <pre-rebase-op>` rewinds the whole rebase in one step, putting main back where it was.

Ready to fire?

```
jj rebase -b main -o init-clone-refactor -R . --ignore-immutable
```
